    r=[0]*(len(a)+len(b)-1)

    for i in range(len(a)):

        for j in range(len(b)):

    return r

* No three points are co-linear.

N=R=Q=0 shows the end of input.

Px1 Px1

   183	            else:
   184	                elements = self.get_children(w, klass, attr, value, id_, elements)
   185	
   186	        return elements

   188	    def set_children(
   189	        self, children: Union[str, v.VuetifyWidget, list], position: str = "first"
   190	    ) -> Self:
   191	        """
   192	        Insert input children in self children within given position.
   193	
   194	        Args:
   195	            children: the list of children to add to the widget. It can also be a list (str and DOMWidgets are accepted)
   196	            position: whether to insert as first or last element. ["first", "last"]
   197	        """
   198	        if not isinstance(children, list):
   199	            children = [children]
   200	
   201	        new_childrens = self.children[:]
   202	
   203	        if position == "first":
   204	            new_childrens = children + new_childrens
   205	
   206	        elif position == "last":
   207	            new_childrens = new_childrens + children
   208	
   209	        else:
   210	            raise ValueError(
   211	                f"Position '{position}' is not a valid value. Use 'first' or 'last'"
   212	            )
   213	
   214	        self.children = new_childrens
   215	
   216	        return self
   217	
   218	    @versionadded(version="2.9.0", reason="Tooltip are now integrated to widgets")
   219	    def set_tooltip(self, txt: str = "", **kwargs) -> v.Tooltip:
   220	        """
   221	        Create a tooltip associated with the widget.
   222	
   223	        If the text is not set, the tooltip will be automatically removed. Once the tooltip is set the object variable can be accessed normally but to render the widget, one will need to use :code:`self.with_tooltip` (irreversible).
   224	
   225	        Args:
   226	            txt: anything False (0, False, empty text, None) will lead to the removal of the tooltip. everything else will be used to fill the text area
   227	            kwargs: any options available in a Tooltip widget
   228	
   229	        Returns:
   230	            the tooltip associated with the object
   231	        """
   232	        if isinstance(self.with_tooltip, Tooltip):
   233	            # If it's already created, and there are new kwargs, let's modify it
   234	            [setattr(self.with_tooltip, attr, value) for attr, value in kwargs.items()]
   235	            self.with_tooltip.children = [txt]
   236	            self.with_tooltip.disabled = not bool(txt)
   237	        elif txt != "":
   238	            self.with_tooltip = Tooltip(self, txt, **kwargs)
   239	
   240	        return self
   241

We have to consider all vertices except P, compute the θ that would cause their next contact, choose the minimum θ (smallest angle) to rotate before one has to pivot. 

At that minimal angle (call it θ_rot), the vertices which are touching the circle are the possible candidates for X. Among those vertices V where their solving gives this minimal θ_rot, we select the one further in clockwise direction from P (measured along the circle's edge). The distance is the arc length from P to the candidate vertex's position on the circle (the contact point they reach at that angle) in the clockwise direction.

Wait, but the contact point is when after rotating through angle θ_rot around P, their new position is exactly on the circle, so the contact point is that new rotated point. But since the new contact point must lie on the circle, and rotating P itself fixed, the position of that point on the circle can be calculated. 

Therefore, for a given vertex V and pivot point P, the equation gives possible θ_rot, the angle to rotate so that V's rotated position is on the circle. The minimal such positive θ (the smallest positive solution) is the candidate time. 

The problem is that solving that equation for θ requires solving the equation:

C cosθ + S sinθ = d 

where C, S, d are known. 

We can represent this equation as:

C cosθ + S sinθ = D 

where D = - (a^2 + b^2)/2. 

This can be rewritten using trigonometric identities. Let's let:

K = sqrt( C² + S² )

then, we can write C = K cosφ, S = K sinφ 

Hence,

K cosφ cosθ + K sinφ sinθ = K cos(θ - φ) = D 

Thus:

cos(θ - φ) = D / K 

So for this equation to have solutions, |D| <= K. 

The solutions are:

θ - φ = arccos(<#sep#> 86	    def test_set_children_error(self):

However, the second condition \( f(x) > 1 \) for \( x \geq 1 \) implies that \( f \) cannot be periodic because it would repeat values in the interval \([1, \infty)\), contradicting the requirement that \( f(x) > 1 \) for all \( x \geq 1 \).

### Step 4: Functional Equation Analysis
Given the functional equation:
\[
f(x + f(y)) = y f(xy + 1)
\]
we need to explore if any specific forms of \( f \) can satisfy both conditions.

### Step 5: Testing Simple Functions
#### Testing \( f(x) = \frac{1}{x} \):
Substitute \( f(x) = \frac{1}{x} \) into the functional equation:
\[
f(x + f(y)) = f\left(x + \frac{1}{y}\right) = \frac{y}{xy + 1}
\]
\[
y f(xy + 1) = y \cdot \frac{1}{xy + 1} = \frac{y}{xy + 1}
\]
Both sides are equal, so \( f(x) = \frac{1}{x} \) satisfies the functional equation. However, it does not satisfy the second condition \( f(x) > 1 \) for \( x \geq 1 \) because \( f(x) = \frac{1}{x} \leq 1 \) for \( x \geq 1 \).

### Step 6: Conclusion
Since \( f(x) = \frac{1}{x} \) is the only simple form that satisfies the functional equation but fails the second condition, and no other forms seem to work without leading to contradictions, we conclude that no function \( f: \mathbb{R}^+ \to \mathbb{R}^+ \) can satisfy both conditions simultaneously.

## Phase 5. FIX ANALYSIS

### 5.1 Problem Statement
The `get_children` method in `sepal_ui/sepalwidgets/sepalwidget.py` is not truly recursive. When a widget matches the search criteria, it adds the widget to the results but does not continue to recursively search within that widget's children.

### 5.2 Problem Location
File: `/testbed/sepal_ui/sepalwidgets/sepalwidget.py`
Method: `get_children` (lines 120-186)
Specific issue: Lines 180-184

### 5.3 How the Test Reproduces the Issue
The test creates a structure where:
- Cards with id=0,1,2,3,4 each contain 5 alerts with id=0,1,2,3,4
- When searching for id=3, it should find:
  - 1 alert from card 0 (id=3)
  - 1 alert from card 1 (id=3) 
  - 1 alert from card 2 (id=3)

### 5.4 Best Practices for the Fix
- Maintain backward compatibility
- Ensure proper recursion without infinite loops
- Handle edge cases (None children, non-widget children)
- Keep the same function signature and return type

### 5.5 How to Fix the Problem
The fix is to change the logic so that:
1. Always check if the current widget matches criteria and add it if it does
2. Always recursively search in children regardless of whether current widget matches

The current logic:
```python
if is_klass and is_val:
    elements.append(w)
else:
    elements = self.get_children(w, klass, attr, value, id_, elements)
```

Should become:
```python
if is_klass and is_val:
    elements.append(w)
# Always recursively search children
elements = self.get_children(w, klass, attr, value, id_, elements)
```

(P.x a + P.y b)^2 + ( - P.x b + P.y a )^2 

Expand:

P.x² a² + 2 P.x a P.y b + P.y² b^2 

+ 

 P.x² b² - 2 P.x b P.y a + P.y² a^2 

Adding terms together: 

P.x^2 (a² +b^2) + P.y^2 (a² +b^2 ) + 2 P.x a P.y b - 2 P.x b P.y a 

The cross terms cancel (the last two terms). Thus overall:

(P.x^2 + P.y^2) (a^2 + b^2) 

Since P.x² + P.y^2 = R².

Therefore:

C² + S^2 = R² ( a² + b² )

Thus K = sqrt(C² + S²) = R * sqrt( a² + b² )

    31	    :target: https://pypi.org/project/sepal-ui<#sep#> chosen randomly and uniformly inside a circle, we need to consider the conditions under which the origin is contained within the triangle.

### Key Insight:
The origin \( O \) will be inside the triangle \( ABC \) if and only if the three points \( A, B, \) and \( C \) do not all lie within any semicircle of the circle. This is because:
- If all three points lie within a semicircle, the origin will be outside that semicircle and thus not inside the triangle.
- Conversely, if the points are not all within a semicircle, the triangle must contain the origin.

### Step-by-Step Solution:

1. **Angular Distribution:**
   - Consider the angles \( \theta_A, \theta_B, \theta_C \) of the three points \( A, B, \) and \( C \) with respect to the center \( O \). These angles are uniformly distributed between \( 0 \) and \( 2\pi \).
   - The radial positions (distances from the center) do not affect whether the points lie within a semicircle. Therefore, we only need to consider the angular distribution.

2. **Probability Calculation:**
   - For points on the circumference of a circle, the probability that three points all lie within a semicircle is \( \frac{3}{4} \).
   - This result holds for points inside the circle as well because the angular distribution remains the same.

3. **Complementary Probability:**
   - The probability that the three points do not all lie within a semicircle is the complement of the probability that they all lie within a semicircle.
   - Therefore, the probability that the three points do not all lie within a semicircle is \( 1 - \frac{3}{4} = \frac{1}{4} \).

### Conclusion:
The probability \( q \) that the origin \( O \) is inside the triangle \( ABC \) is \(\boxed{\dfrac{1}{4}}\).<#sep#>1 and theta2 modulo 2pi.

def compute_theta_parameters(a,b,Px,Py):

    # given vector (a,b) from P to V.

    C = Px *a + Py * b 

    S = -Px * b + Py * a 

    K = sqrt( C*C + S*S )

    D = - (a*a + b*b)/2. 

    assert K > 1e-6, "small rotation not handled" # but given problem has R >=1.

    delta = acos( D / K )

    phi = math.atan2(S, C)

    theta1 = phi + delta 

    theta2 = phi - delta 

    return theta1, theta2

    73	:code:`sepal_ui` is a lib designed to create elegant python based dashboard in the `SEPAL environment <https://sepal.io/>`__. It is designed on top of the amazing `ipyvuetify <https://ipyvuetify.readthedocs.io/en/latest/introduction.html>`_ library and will help developer to easily create interface for their workflows.
    74	By using this libraries, you'll ensure a robust and unified interface for your scripts and a easy and complete integration into the SEPAL dashboard of application.

= a³ + b³ + c³ - 3abc + (a + b + c)(ab + bc + ca)
= (a + b + c)(a² + b² + c² - ab - bc - ca) + (a + b + c)(ab + bc + ca)
= (a + b + c)(a² + b² + c² - ab - bc - ca + ab + bc + ca)
= (a + b + c)(a² + b² + c²).

Therefore, the factorized form is (a + b + c)(a² + b² + c²).

Let me check this with a simple example. Let’s take a = 1, b = 1, c = 1.

Original expression: 1 + 1 + 1 + (2)(2)(2) - 2(1) = 3 + 8 - 2 = 9.

Factored expression: (1+1+1)(1 +1 +1) = 3*3 = 9. Correct.

Another test: a=2, b=3, c=4.

Original expression: 8 + 27 + 64 + (5)(7)(6) - 2*24.

Calculating step by step:

8 + 27 + 64 = 99.

(5)(7) = 35, 35*6 = 210.

So 99 + 210 = 309.

Then subtract 2*24 = 48. 309 - 48 = 261.

Now the factored expression: (2 + 3 + 4)(4 + 9 + 16) = 9*(<#sep#> after rotation, which is on the circle O (since we evaluated at θ_rot_v), we can compute the angle of X (angle_x), and then the clockwise distance.

Once we have that distance, the point with the largest such distance would be selected.

Thus for each candidate point V which has the minimal θ_rot_v, compute their rotated position X (the new pivot point). 

To compute the angle_x of X's position:

The point X is the rotation of V by theta_rot around P.

The formula for rotation:

X's coordinates after rotation:

x = P.x + (a * cos(theta_rot_v) - b * sin(theta_rot_v) )

y = P.y + (a * sin(theta_rot_v) + b * cos(theta_rot_v) )

Thus, (X.x, X.y) must lie on the circle (e.g. x² + y² == R^2 is satisfied by our equation.

Then, the angle_x is the angle from the origin to X, which is arctan2(Y, X). But normalized between 0 and 2pi.

Wait since the circle O is the original xy plane's origin-centered circle.

The angle of X is angle_x = math.atan2( X.y, X.x ), but we need to adjust to [0,2pi).

Thus:

def compute_angle(x,y):

    ang = math.atan2(y,x)

    if ang <0:

        ang += 2*math.pi

    return ang 

then:

angle_x = compute_angle(X.x, X.y)

angle_p is the angle of P:

angle_p = compute_angle(P.x, P.y)

Then, the arc length clockwise is (angle_p - angle_x) mod 2pi (positive distance).

Thus the clockwise distance is (angle_p - angle_x + 2*math.pi ) % (2*math.pi )

Which is the actual arc length divided by R (because R * that angle in radians is the length).

To compare two points, their clockwise_angular_distances can be compared.

Now, among candidates with minimal theta_rot_v, selects the one with maximal clockwise angular distance.

So for the current pivot P's next pivot:

The next pivot X must be the candidate vertex V where:

V has minimal theta_rot_v (equals the minimal among all vertices).

Among those vertices with minimal theta_rot_v, select the one whose corresponding X has maximum (clockwise_angular_distance).

Once that is chosen, the new pivot P' becomes the new pivot, and the X is added to the output.

Thus, the steps:

For each step of the process until we have Q points:

Current pivot is P.

For every vertex V not equal to P:

1. Compute theta_rot_v as min(theta1_n, theta2_n).

These steps compute the minimal angles needed for each vertex.

2. Among all these vertices, find the minimal theta_rot value.

3. Collect all Vertex candidates that have theta_rot_v equal to the minimal theta_rot.

4. For each of these candidates, compute their new X = the position after rotating V by the minimal theta_rot.

5. For each such candidate X, compute their angle_x, and the clockwise distance from P's angle_p to X's angle_x.

6. Pick the vertex among these candidates where the clockwise distance is the largest.

That vertex's X will be the next pivot point. 

Then, we add that new pivot to our output list (assuming we're still under Q), and repeat the process with the new P = new pivot.

So, this is a loop that proceeds until Q selections are made or it loops indefinitely? The problem says Q can be up to 100.

But how do we track whether the same pivots are getting repeated? Since the problem says that the successive points must be different. Thus the loop will eventually repeat but meets the termination when we have enough Q points.

Wait but according to the problem's input constraints, it's possible the polygon has a movement path which eventually goes in a closed loop; so before exceeding Q we could stop.

Thus implementing this requires:

Managing the current pivot, then performing these steps until Q points are acquired.

Thus, the algorithm is manageable once we can compute all these values.

         g. the new pivot P is that X.

      3. repeat until Q entries.

def find_initial_pivot(points, R_squared):

    for i in range(len(points)):

        x,y = points[i]

        if abs(x**2 + y**2 - R_squared) < 1e-6:

            return i 

    return -1

</think>To factorize the expression \(a^3 + b^3 + c^3 + (a + b)(b + c)(c + a) - 2abc\), we will use known algebraic identities and simplify step-by-step.

1. **Expand the product \((a + b)(b + c)(c + a)\):**

   We use the identity:
   \[
   (a + b)(b + c)(c + a) = (a + b + c)(ab + bc + ca) - abc
   \]

2. **Substitute this identity into the original expression:**

   \[
   a^3 + b^3 + c^3 + (a + b)(b + c)(c + a) - 2abc = a^3 + b^3 + c^3 + [(a + b + c)(ab + bc + ca) - abc] - 2abc
   \]

3. **Simplify the terms involving \(abc\):**

   \[
   a^3 + b^3 + c^3 + (a + b + c)(ab + bc + ca) - abc - 2abc = a^3 + b^3 + c^3 + (a + b + c)(ab + bc + ca) - 3abc
   \]

4. **Use the identity for the sum of cubes:**

   \[
   a^3 + b^3 + c^3 - 3abc = (a + b + c)(a^2 + b^2 + c^2 - ab - bc - ca)
   \]

5. **Substitute this identity into the expression:**

   \[
   (a + b + c)(a^2 + b^2 + c^2 - ab - bc - ca) + (a + b + c)(ab + bc + ca)
   \]

6. **Factor out \((a + b + c)\):**

   \[
   (a + b + c)\left[(a^2 + b^2 + c^2 - ab - bc - ca) + (ab + bc + ca)\right]
   \]

7. **Simplify inside the brackets:**

   \[
   (a + b + c)(a^2 + b^2 + c^2 - ab - bc - ca + ab + bc + ca) = (a + b + c)(a^2 + b^2 + c^2)
   \]

Thus, the factorized form of the expression is:
\[
\boxed{(a + b + c)(a^2 + b^2 + c^2)}

The insurgents suppose that this polygon is developing new deadly weapon. The polygon consists of n missile silos connected by bidirectional underground passages. The passages are linked to laboratories where research is conducted. Naturally, the passages are guarded severely: the passage between silos i and j is patrolled by ci, j war droids.

The insurgents studied the polygon plan and noticed its unusual structure. As it turned out, for any k-element set of silos S there is exactly one silo that is directly connected by a passage with each silo from S (we'll call this silo adjacent with S). Having considered that, the insurgents decided to act as follows:

  1. they choose a k-element set of silos S; 
  2. a group of scouts lands from the air into each silo from S; 
  3. each group moves along the corresponding passage to the silo, adjacent with S (as the scouts move, they check out the laboratories and watch for any signs of weapon blueprints); 
  4. in the silo, adjacent with S, the groups get on the ship and fly away. 



The danger of the operation is the total number of droids that patrol the passages through which the scouts will go. The danger of the operation obviously only depends on the way to choose set S. The insurgents haven't yet decided on the exact silos to send the scouts to. However, they already want to start preparing the weapons for the scout groups. To do that, the insurgents need to know the mathematical average of the dangers of the operations that correspond to all possible ways to choose set S. Solve this problem to help the insurgents protect the ideals of the Republic!
Input

The first line contains two integers n and k (2 ≤ n ≤ 2000, 1 ≤ k ≤ n - 1) — the number of silos and the number of scout groups, correspondingly. The next n - 1 lines describe the polygon plan: the i-th of these lines contains n - i integers ci, i + 1, ci, i + 2, ..., ci, n — the number of droids that patrol the corresponding passages (-1 ≤ ci, j ≤ 109; if ci, j =  -1, then silos i and j don't have a passage between them). All passages are bidirectional, that is, we can assume that ci, j = cj, i. No passages connect a silo with itself. It is guaranteed that the polygon plan meets the conditions of the problem statement.

Output

Print the average danger of the scouting operation, rounded down to an integer. Note that at the given limits the answer to the problem always fits into the standard integer 64-bit data type.

Please do not use the %lld specifier to write 64-bit integers in С++. It is preferred to use the cout stream or the %I64d specifier.

Examples

Input

6 1
-1 -1 -1 8 -1
-1 5 -1 -1
-1 -1 3
-1 -1
-1


Output

5


Input

3 2
10 0
11


Output

14

Note

In the first sample there are 6 one-element sets of silos. For sets {1}, {5} the operation danger will equal 8, for sets {3}, {6} — 3, for sets {2}, {4} — 5. The mathematical average equals <image>.

In the second sample there are 3 two-elements sets of silos: {1, 3} (danger equals 21), {1, 2} (danger equals 11), {2, 3} (danger equals 10). The average operation danger equals <image>.

   120	    def get_children(
   121	        self,
   122	        widget: Optional[v.VuetifyWidget] = None,
   123	        klass: Type[v.VuetifyWidget] = v.VuetifyWidget,
   124	        attr: str = "",
   125	        value: str = "",
   126	        id_: str = "",
   127	        elements: Optional[list] = None,
   128	    ) -> list:
   129	        r"""
   130	        Recursively search for every element matching the specifications.
   131	
   132	        multiple parameters can be used to search for matching elements. no error is raised if nothing is found.
   133	
   134	        Args:
   135	            widget: the widget to search into
   136	            klass: the vuetify widget class to look for . Leave empty for any.
   137	            attr: the attribute to look at. leave empty for no search
   138	            value: the value of the attr. ignored if attr is not set
   139	            elements: the list used to store found elements
   140	
   141	        Returns:
   142	            List containing all matching elements
   143	
   144	        Retrieve all children elements that matches with the given id\_.
   145	
   146	        Args:
   147	            id\_ (str, optional): attribute id to compare with.
   148	
   149	        Returns:
   150	            list with all mathing elements if there are more than one, otherwise will return the matching element.
   151	
   152	        """
   153	        # id_ was the previous variable it should continue working in this implementation
   154	        # remove kwargs when this will be deprecated
   155	        if id_ != "":
   156	            attr, value = ("id", id_)
   157	            warnings.warn(
   158	                '"id_" is a deprecated argument, use an ("attr", "value") pair instead',
   159	                DeprecationWarning,
   160	            )
   161	
   162	        # init the element list
   163	        elements = [] if elements is None else elements
   164	
   165	        # init the widget
   166	        widget = self if widget is None else widget
   167	
   168	        for w in widget.children:
   169	
   170	            # exit if children is not a widget (str, DOM objects.. etc)
   171	            if not isinstance(w, v.VuetifyWidget):
   172	                continue
   173	
   174	            # compare the widget with requirements
   175	            # using "niet" as default so that result is True if attr is Falsy
   176	            # "niet" is very unlikely to be used compared to None, False, "none"...
   177	            is_klass = isinstance(w, klass)
   178	            is_val = w.attributes.get(attr, "niet") == value if attr and value else True
   179	
   180	            # asumption: searched element won't be nested inside one another
   181	            if is_klass and is_val:
   182	                elements.append(w)
   183	            else:
   184	                elements = self.get_children(w, klass, attr, value, id_, elements)
   185	
   186	        return elements
   187	
   188	    def set_children(
   189	        self, children: Union[str, v.VuetifyWidget, list], position: str = "first"
   190	    ) -> Self:
   191	        """
   192	        Insert input children in self children within given position.
   193	
   194	        Args:
   195	            children: the list of children to add to the widget. It can also be a list (str and DOMWidgets are accepted)
   196	            position: whether to insert as first or last element. ["first", "last"]
   197	        """
   198	        if not isinstance(children, list):
   199	            children = [children]
   200	
   201	        new_childrens = self.children[:]
   202	
   203	        if position == "first":
   204	            new_childrens = children + new_childrens
   205	
   206	        elif position == "last":
   207	            new_childrens = new_childrens + children
   208	
   209	        else:
   210	            raise ValueError(
   211	                f"Position '{position}' is not a valid value. Use 'first' or 'last'"
   212	            )
   213	
   214	        self.children = new_childrens
   215	
   216	        return self
   217

   226	            txt: anything False (0, False, empty text, None) will lead to the removal of the tooltip. everything else will be used to fill the text area
   227	            kwargs: any options available in a Tooltip widget
   228	
   229	        Returns:
   230	            the tooltip associated with the object
   231	        """
   232	        if isinstance(self.with_tooltip, Tooltip):
   233	            # If it's already created, and there are new kwargs, let's modify it
   234	            [setattr(self.with_tooltip, attr, value) for attr, value in kwargs.items()]
   235	            self.with_tooltip.children = [txt]

If this is the case, then the total danger is equal to the sum for each edge e of c_e multiplied by (C( du -1, k-1) + C(dv-1, k-1 )).

Therefore, if that is true, the average would be this total divided by the number of k-element subsets (i.e., C(n, k)).

So the problem reduces to:

1. For each node v, compute its degree d_v (the number of friends it has, which is the number of edges it is part of) which is given by the count of non -1 entries in the matrix (since if the input has ci,j = -1 then there's no edge between i and j).

2. For each edge between u and v, compute term (comb(d_u-1, k-1) + comb(d_v-1, k-1)), multiply it by the edge's weight, add all together.

3. Divide the total sum by C(n, k) and then floor the result.

So the key is, for each edge, compute the combination terms from their degrees and sum appropriately.

assistant: Now I can see the issue! Looking at the `get_children` method (lines 120-186), I can see that it's supposed to be recursive (the docstring says "Recursively search for every element matching the specifications"), but there's a logic error in the implementation.

Let me analyze the problem:

1. Line 168: `for w in widget.children:` - iterates through direct children
2. Lines 174-182: Check if the current child matches the criteria and add it to elements
3. Line 184: If it doesn't match, it recursively calls itself on that child

The problem is in the logic: when a child matches the criteria, it's added to elements, but the function doesn't recursively search within that matching child for more nested matches. The recursion only happens when the child doesn't match.

This means that when it finds a Card with id=3, it adds the Card to the results but doesn't search within that Card for its children that might also match (like the Alert with id=3 inside that Card).

Let me look at the test to confirm my understanding:

## Phase 5. FIX ANALYSIS



tool: Your thought has been logged.

assistant: ### 5.1 Problem Statement
The `get_children` method in `SepalWidget` class is not recursively searching within matching elements. When a widget matches the search criteria, the method adds it to results but doesn't search within that widget for additional nested matches.

### 5.2 Problem Location  
File: `/testbed/sepal_ui/sepalwidgets/sepalwidget.py`, lines 181-184

### 5.3 How Test Reproduces Issue
The test creates a nested structure where a Card with id=3 contains Alerts, one of which also has id=3. The current implementation finds the Card but doesn't search within it to find the nested Alert.

### 5.4 Best Practices
- Maintain API compatibility
- Preserve all existing functionality  
- Make minimal, focused changes
- Ensure proper recursion termination

### 5.5 Solution
Change the logic to always recursively search within child widgets, regardless of whether they match the criteria themselves. The current "else" condition should be removed so that matching elements are both added to results AND recursively searched.

Implementation code steps:

Compute C(n,k):

if T is k-1, so binomial(n, k) can be computed as:

def combN(n, k):

    if k >n: return 0

    if k ==0 or k ==n: return 1

    k = min(k, n-k)

    res =1

    for i in range(1, k+1):

        res *= (n -k +i)

        res //=i

    return res

So that's how we can compute the binomial coefficient.

</think>To solve the problem, we need to find the ratio \(\frac{R}{R_0}\) where \(R\) is the circumradius of the circle passing through points \(B\), \(C\), \(P\), and \(Q\), and \(R_0\) is the circumradius of triangle \(ABC\).

### Step-by-Step Solution:

1. **Understanding the Geometry**:
   - In triangle \(ABC\), the angle bisector at \(A\), the altitude from \(B\) to \(CA\), and the altitude from \(C\) to \(AB\) form a triangle \(\triangle T\).
   - The orthocenter of \(\triangle T\) is the midpoint of segment \(PQ\), where \(P\) and \(Q\) are points on lines \(AB\) and \(AC\), respectively.

2. **Key Observations**:
   - The orthocenter of \(\triangle T\) is a crucial point in the problem. It suggests a symmetrical relationship involving the midpoint of \(PQ\).
   - The circumradius \(R\) of the circle through \(B\), \(C\), \(P\), and \(Q\) is related to the circumradius \(R_0\) of triangle \(ABC\).

3. **Properties of the Nine-Point Circle**:
   - The nine-point circle of a triangle passes through the midpoints of the sides, the feet of the altitudes, and the midpoints of the segments from each vertex to the orthocenter.
   - The radius of the nine-point circle is half the circumradius of the original triangle.

4. **Relating \(R\) to \(R_0\)**:
   - Given that the midpoint of \(PQ\) is the orthocenter of \(\triangle T\), and considering the properties of the nine-point circle, we can infer that the circle through \(B\), \(C\), \(P\), and \(Q\) is essentially a scaled version of the nine-point circle.
   - Therefore, the circumradius \(R\) of this circle is half the circumradius \(R_0\) of triangle \(ABC\).

### Conclusion:
The ratio of the circumradius \(R\) of the circle passing through \(B\), \(C\), \(P\), and \(Q\) to the circumradius \(R_0\) of triangle \(ABC\) is:

I remember that the osculating plane at a point on a curve is the plane that best approximates the curve at that point. It's defined by the tangent and normal vectors, right? Wait, no, the osculating plane is actually spanned by the tangent vector T and the normal vector N. The binormal vector B is perpendicular to the osculating plane. So the osculating plane can be found using the binormal vector as the normal vector of the plane. Let me check that again. 

Yes, the osculating plane at a point on a curve is the plane that contains the tangent vector and the principal normal vector. The binormal vector B is the cross product of T and N, so B is perpendicular to the osculating plane. Therefore, the equation of the osculating plane can be written as B ⋅ (X - P) = 0, where B is the binormal vector at that point and P is the point of contact. 

So to find the osculating plane at point P(1, 1/4, 0), I need to:

1. Find the parameter value t that corresponds to the point P.
2. Compute the tangent vector T at that t.
3. Compute the normal vector N at that t.
4. Find the binormal vector B = T × N.
5. Then use B as the normal vector to the plane passing through P.

y = t/π
z = cos(2t)

z = cos(2*(π/4)) = cos(π/2) = 0. Correct.

   <#sep#> -2|
|-4  0    0|

= i*(1/π*0 - (-2)*0) - j*(0*0 - (-2)*(-4)) + k*(0*0 - 1/π*(-4))
= i*(0 - 0) - j*(0 - 8) + k*(0 + 4/π)
= 0i - (-8)j + (4/π)k
= 8j + (4/π)k

So the cross product is (0, 8, 4/π). Therefore, this is the normal vector to the osculating plane.

Thus, the equation of the osculating plane is:

(0, 8, 4/π) ⋅ (X - P) = 0

Where X = (x, y, z), and P = (1, 1/4, 0).

Expanding this:

0*(x - 1) + 8*(y - 1/4) + (4/π)*(z - 0) = 0

Simplify:

8(y - 1/4) + (4/π)z = 0

Multiply out the terms:

8y - 2 + (4/π)z = 0

Bring constants to the other side:

8y + (4/π)z = 2

We can divide both sides by 2 to simplify:

4y + (2/π)z = 1

Alternatively, we can write the equation as:

4y + (2/π)z = 1

Alternatively, if we want to avoid fractions, we can multiply both sides by π to get:

4π y + 2 z = π

But perhaps the first simplified version is fine. Let me check if that's correct.

Alternatively, let me verify the cross product calculation again to make sure.

r' is (0, 1/π, -2)

r'' is (-4, 0, 0)

Cross product:

i component: (1/π * 0) - (-2 * 0) = 0 - 0 = 0

j component: - [0 * 0 - (-2)*(-4)] = - [0 - 8] = - (-8) = 8

k component: 0 * 0 - (1/π)*(-4) = 0 + 4/π

So cross product is (0, 8, 4/π). Correct.

Then the plane equation:

Thus, N = (r''(t)/|r'(t)|) / (|r''(t)| / |r'(t)| ) ) = r''(t)/|r''(t)|.

|i    j       k|
|0     a       b|

So here's the idea: start with "1" (since leading zeros aren't allowed), then at each step, append either a '0' or '1' to the current number. Check each new number formed to see if it's divisible by k. The first such number is our answer.

- Start with remainder r = 1 (since starting with "1").

- Else, we branch: add a '0' (multiply by 10 and add 0) or '1' (multiply by 10 and add 1). The remainders of these operations can be computed based on previous remainder.

Similarly for 1: (r * 10 +1) mod k.

Thus, the BFS should track these remainders, ensuring that we don't revisit the same remainder (since cycles would be infinite otherwise). Because once a remainder is visited again, there's no need since longer numbers wouldn't help in finding a smaller number. So the key is to avoid cycles.

Therefore, the BFS approach would track the current remainder, avoiding revisiting remainders once they've been seen before. The first time a remainder is 0 is the minimal length, so this approach should work.

The BFS proceeds by dequeuing a remainder r, then checking the two possibilities (appended 0 and 1):

For each possible next remainder (r0 and r1):

Compute r0 = (r * 10 + 0) mod k 

r1 = (r *10 +1) mod k

The problem is, how do you track the actual number? Because without the digits, when you reach 0, you can't know the digits. Oh, but perhaps we can track the current path (digits), but that's going to be memory-heavy for large numbers. Alternatively, can we reconstruct the path when the remainder is 0?

Alternatively, perhaps we can track the path as part of the state in BFS, so the queue would hold (remainder, current_number), and once the remainder is 0, we can return the current_number. Since BFS works in order of increasing length (since we process nodes in the order they were added, and each step adds a digit), the first occurrence where remainder is 0 is the minimal-length number. So this approach works.

But this requires storing the current number as a string or integer, which can be memory-consuming. However, for the purposes of code golf,<#sep#>palwidgets/alert.py

The osculating plane is \boxed{4\pi y + 2z = \pi}.
</think>To find the osculating plane of the curve \( C \) defined by the parametric equation \(\vec{r}(t) = (\sin(2t), \frac{t}{\pi}, \cos(2t))\) at the point \( P(1, \frac{1}{4}, 0) \):

1. **Determine the parameter \( t \) corresponding to point \( P \)**:
   - The coordinates of \( P \) are given by:
     \[
     \sin(2t) = 1, \quad \frac{t}{\pi} = \frac{1}{4}, \quad \cos(2t) = 0
     \]
   - Solving these equations, we find \( t = \frac{\pi}{4} \).

2. **Compute the first and second derivatives of \(\vec{r}(t)\)**:
   - The first derivative is:
     \[
     \vec{r}'(t) = (2\cos(2t), \frac{1}{\pi}, -2\sin(2t))
     \]
   - Evaluating at \( t = \frac{\pi}{4} \):
     \[
     \vec{r}'\left(\frac{\pi}{4}\right) = (2\cos(\frac{\pi}{2}), \frac{1}{\pi}, -2\sin(\frac{\pi}{2})) = (0, \frac{1}{\pi}, -2)
     \]
   - The second derivative is:
     \[
     \vec{r}''(t) = (-4\sin(2t), 0, -4\cos(2t))
     \]
   - Evaluating at \( t = \frac{\pi}{4} \):
     \[
     \vec{r}''\left(\frac{\pi}{4}\right) = (-4\sin(\frac{\pi}{2}), 0, -4\cos(\frac{\pi}{2})) = (-4, 0, 0)
     \]

3. **Compute the cross product \(\vec{r}'\left(\frac{\pi}{4}\right) \times \vec{r}''\left(\frac{\pi}{4}\right)\)**:
   - The cross product is:
     \[
     \vec{r}'\left(\frac{\pi}{4}\right) \times \vec{r}''\left(\frac{\pi}{4}\right) = \begin{vmatrix}
     \mathbf{i} & \mathbf{j} & \mathbf{k} \\
     0 & \frac{1}{\pi} & -2 \\
     -4 & 0 & 0
     \end{vmatrix} = (0, 8, \frac{4}{\pi})
     \]

4. **Form the equation of the osculating plane**:
   - The normal vector to the plane is \((0, 8, \frac{4}{\pi})\).
   - Using the point \( P(1, \frac{1}{4}, 0) \), the plane equation is:
     \[
     0(x - 1) + 8\left(y - \frac{1}{4}\right) + \frac{4}{\pi}(z - 0) = 0
     \]
   - Simplifying:
     \[
     8\left(y - \frac{1}{4}\right) + \frac{4}{\pi}z = 0 \implies 8y - 2 + \frac{4}{\pi}z = 0 \implies 8y + \frac{4}{\pi}z = 2
     \]
   - Dividing by 2:
     \[
     4y + \frac{2}{\pi}z = 1
     \]
   - Multiplying by \(\pi\) to clear the fraction:
     \[
     4\pi y + 2z = \pi
     \]

Thus, the osculating plane of the curve \( C \) at the point \( P(1, \frac{1}{4}, 0) \) is \(\boxed{4\pi y + 2z = \pi}\).<#sep#> can we reconstruct the path when the remainder is 0?

def find_binary_multiple(k):
    from collections import deque
    visited = set()
    q = deque()
    q.append( (1, '1') )
    visited.add(1)
    if k ==1: return 1
    while q:
        rem, s = q.popleft()
        if rem ==0:
            return int(s)
        # Generate next steps
        new_rem = (rem*10 +0) %k
        new_s = s + '0'
        if new_rem not in visited:
            visited.add(new_rem)
            q.append( (new_rem, new_s) )
        new_rem = (rem*10 +1)%k
        new_s1 = s+'1'
        if new_rem not in visited:
            visited.add(new_rem)
            q.append( (new_rem, new_s1) )
    return something # unreachable?

Alternatively, maybe when two variables approach each other and the third is small. Let me try taking one variable approaching zero.

Suppose z approaches 0. Since x, y, z >0, but z approaching 0. Then, the condition \(xy + yz + zx = xyz\) becomes approximately xy ≈ 0, but since x and y are positive, this can't happen. Wait, if z approaches 0, then the left-hand side (xy + yz + zx) approaches xy, and the right-hand side approaches 0. So, unless xy approaches 0, which would require either x or y approaching 0. But x, y, z are all positive, so if z approaches 0, we need to have xy approaching 0 as well, which would require either x or y approaching 0. However, if both x and y approach 0, then their product approaches 0, but then xy + yz + zx approaches 0, which equals xyz (which also approaches 0). So maybe this case is possible but perhaps tricky. Let's consider z approaching 0, x approaching 0, and y approaching infinity such that xy = constant. Wait, this might get complicated. Let me try to parametrize.

Suppose z approaches 0. Then, from the condition \(xy + yz + zx = xyz\), we can write:

xy + z(x + y) = xyz ⇒ xy ≈ xyz when z is near 0. But then, as z approaches 0, unless xy approaches 0, which would require either x or y approaches 0, but in that case, z(x + y) would be negligible. Alternatively, if we set x = z and let z approach 0. Then, x = z →0, and let y be something.

Wait, let me try setting x = z. Let x = z = t, and t approaches 0. Then the condition becomes:

xy + yz + zx = xyz ⇒ t y + y t + t² = t² y.

Simplifying, 2 t y + t² = t² y. Divide both sides by t (since t >0):

2 y + t = t y ⇒ 2 y = t y - t ⇒ 2 y = t(y - 1). Then, solving for y: y = [t(y - 1)]/2. Hmm, this is a bit recursive, but perhaps rearranging:

2y = t(y -1) ⇒ 2y - t y = -t ⇒ y(2 - t) = -t ⇒ y = (-t)/(2 - t). But since t approaches 0 from the positive side, the denominator is approximately 2, so y ≈ (-t)/2, but y must be positive. This gives a negative y, which contradicts y >0. Therefore, this suggests that taking x = z = t approaching 0 isn't possible, as it would require y to be negative. Therefore, maybe when z approaches 0, x or y can't approach zero. Alternatively, perhaps another approach.

Alternatively, consider the case where two variables are equal and the third is determined by the condition. Let's suppose that x = y, and then express z in terms of x.

Let x = y. Then, the condition becomes:

x² + 2x z = x² z. Let's solve for z.

x² z - 2x z - x² =0 ⇒ z(x² - 2x) = x² ⇒ z = x² / (x² - 2x) = x / (x - 2), provided x ≠2.

Since z must be positive, the denominator x - 2 must have the same sign as the numerator x. Since x >0, denominator x - 2 must be positive as well, so x >2. Therefore, x must be greater than 2.

So, z = x/(x -2). Now, we can express x + y + z = 2x + z = 2x + x/(x -2). Similarly, xy + yz + zx = x² + 2x z = x² + 2x*(x/(x -2)) = x² + 2x²/(x -2) = x²(1 + 2/(x -2)) = x²*(x -2 + 2)/(x -2) ) = x²*(x)/(x -2) ) = x³/(x -2).

So, let me write the inequality in terms of x. The original inequality is:

(2x + x/(x -2))² -81 ≥ k*(x³/(x -2) -27).

We need to find the maximum k such that this holds for all x >2.

So, if we can express this inequality as k ≤ [ (2x + x/(x -2))² -81 ] / [x³/(x -2) -27] for all x >2. Then, the minimal value of the right-hand side over x >2 would be the maximum k.

Therefore, our task reduces to finding the infimum of the function f(x) = [ (2x + x/(x -2))² -81 ] / [x³/(x -2) -27] over x >2, and that infimum would be the maximum k such that the inequality holds.

This seems manageable. Let's compute f(x):

First, let's simplify the numerator and denominator.

Numerator: (2x + x/(x -2))² -81.

Let me combine the terms:

2x + x/(x -2) = x[2 + 1/(x -2)] = x[ (2(x -2) +1)/ (x -2) ) ] = x[ (2x -4 +1)/(x -2) ) ] = x(2x -3)/(x -2).

Therefore, numerator: [x(2x -3)/(x -2)]² -81.

Denominator: x³/(x -2) -27 = (x³ -27(x -2))/ (x -2) ) = [x³ -27x +54]/(x -2).

So, f(x) = [x²(2x -3)^2/(x -2)^2 -81] / [ (x³ -27x +54)/(x -2) ) ].

Simplify numerator of f(x):

= [x²(2x -3)^2 -81(x -2)^2 ] / (x -2)^2

So, f(x) = [x²(2x -3)^2 -81(x -2)^2 ] / (x -2)^2 * [ (x -2)/(x³ -27x +54) ) ]

= [x²(2x -3)^2 -81(x -2)^2 ] / [ (x -2)(x³ -27x +54) ) ]

This looks complicated. Maybe expanding the numerator would help.

First, compute x²(2x -3)^2:

Let’s expand (2x -3)^2 = 4x² -12x +9. Then, x²*(4x² -12x +9) = 4x⁴ -12x³ +9x².

Then, 81(x -2)^2 =81(x² -4x +4) =81x² -324x +324.

So, numerator of f(x) becomes:

4x⁴ -12x³ +9x² -81x² +324x -324 =4x⁴ -12x³ -72x² +324x -324.

Factor numerator:

4x⁴ -12x³ -72x² +324x -324.

Hmm, perhaps factor out a 4 from the first two terms, but maybe not. Let me check if x=3 is a root. Let's plug x=3:

4*(81) -12*(27) -72*(9) +324*(3) -324 =

324 -324 -648 +972 -324 =

(324 -324) =0; (-648 +972)= 324; 324 -324=0. So x=3 is a root. Therefore, (x-3) is a factor.

def f(k):

    while r:

from collections import deque as q
def f(k):
 v={1};r=q([(1,"1")])
 while r:
  a,s=r.popleft()
  if a==0:return s
  for d in(0,1):
   m=(a*10+d)%k
   if m not in v:
    v.add(m)
    r.append((m,s+'01'[d]))

Wait this uses default arguments for v and q. 

Wait in Python, using default arguments for mutable objects (like v and list) has some problem because they are created once. Wait, but when redefining the function, the defaults can mess up with prior runs. However, in code golf, if the function is called once between each test case (as in the online judge, maybe), perhaps it can be okay? Or maybe not. In code golf competition, the function must be written such that it works properly even if called multiple times, which would require that 'v' and 'q' are reinitialized each time. 

Hence, using default arguments for those may create problems when called multiple times, because the v and q would retain their previous values. So that approach may not be safe. 

So requires reinitialization each time. Hence, perhaps the code must reinitialize 'v' and 'q' inside the function each time.

</think>To find the largest real number \( k \) for which the inequality
\[
(x + y + z)^2 - 81 \geq k(xy + yz + zx - 27)
\]
holds true for all \( x, y, z > 0 \) satisfying \( xy + yz + zx = xyz \), we proceed as follows:

First, we consider the case where \( x = y \). The condition \( xy + yz + zx = xyz \) becomes:
\[
x^2 + 2xz = x^2 z \implies z = \frac{x}{x-2} \quad \text{for} \quad x > 2.
\]

Substituting \( x = y \) and \( z = \frac{x}{x-2} \) into the inequality, we need to verify:
\[
\left(2x + \frac{x}{x-2}\right)^2 - 81 \geq k \left( x^2 + 2x \cdot \frac{x}{x-2} - 27 \right).
\]

Simplifying the left-hand side:
\[
2x + \frac{x}{x-2} = \frac{2x(x-2) + x}{x-2} = \frac{2x^2 - 4x + x}{x-2} = \frac{2x^2 - 3x}{x-2}.
\]

Thus,
\[
\left( \frac{2x^2 - 3x}{x-2} \right)^2 - 81.
\]

Simplifying the right-hand side:
\[
x^2 + 2x \cdot \frac{x}{x-2} = x^2 + \frac{2x^2}{x-2} = \frac{x^2(x-2) + 2x^2}{x-2} = \frac{x^3 - 2x^2 + 2x^2}{x-2} = \frac{x^3}{x-2}.
\]

Thus,
\[
\frac{x^3}{x-2} - 27.
\]

The inequality becomes:
\[
\left( \frac{2x^2 - 3x}{x-2} \right)^2 - 81 \geq k \left( \frac{x^3}{x-2} - 27 \right).
\]

Define the function:
\[
f(x) = \frac{\left( \frac{2x^2 - 3x}{x-2} \right)^2 - 81}{\frac{x^3}{x-2} - 27}.
\]

Simplifying the numerator and denominator:
\[
\left( \frac{2x^2 - 3x}{x-2} \right)^2 - 81 = \frac{(2x^2 - 3x)^2 - 81(x-2)^2}{(x-2)^2},
\]
\[
\frac{x^3}{x-2} - 27 = \frac{x^3 - 27(x-2)}{x-2} = \frac{x^3 - 27x + 54}{x-2}.
\]

Thus,
\[
f(x) = \frac{(2x^2 - 3x)^2 - 81(x-2)^2}{(x-2)(x^3 - 27x + 54)}.
\]

Expanding and simplifying:
\[
(2x^2 - 3x)^2 = 4x^4 - 12x^3 + 9x^2,
\]
\[
81(x-2)^2 = 81x^2 - 324x + 324.
\]

Thus,
\[
(2x^2 - 3x)^2 - 81(x-2)^2 = 4x^4 - 12x^3 + 9x^2 - 81x^2 + 324x - 324 = 4x^4 - 12x^3 - 72x^2 + 324x - 324.
\]

Factoring:
\[
4x^4 - 12x^3 - 72x^2 + 324x - 324 = 4(x-3)^2(x^2 + 3x - 9).
\]

Thus,
\[
f(x) = \frac{4(x-3)^2(x^2 + 3x - 9)}{(x-2)(x^3 - 27x + 54)}.
\]

Simplifying further:
\[
x^3 - 27x + 54 = (x-3)(x^2 + 3x - 18).
\]

Thus,
\[
f(x) = \frac{4(x-3)^2(x^2 + 3x - 9)}{(x-2)(x-3)(x^2 + 3x - 18)} = \frac{4(x-3)(x^2 + 3x - 9)}{(x-2)(x^2 + 3x - 18)}.
\]

\]

Evaluating the limit as \( x \to 3 \):
\[
\lim_{x \to 3} f(x) = \frac{4(0)(9 + 9 - 9)}{(1)(9 + 9 - 18)} = \frac{0}{0}.
\]

Using L'Hôpital's Rule:
\[
\lim_{x \to 3} \frac{4(x-3)(x^2 + 3x - 9)}{(x-2)(x^2 + 3x - 18)} = \frac{4(3x^2 - 18)}{(x^2 + 3x - 18) + (x-2)(2x + 3)} = \frac{4(27 - 18)}{9 + 9 - 18 + 1(6 + 3)} = \frac{36}{9} = 4.
\]

Thus, the minimum value of \( f(x) \) is \( \frac{15}{4} \).

Therefore, the largest real number \( k \) is:
\[
\boxed{\frac{15}{4}}.

    41<#sep#>Let \( P \) be a regular \((2n+1)\)-gon in the plane, where \( n \) is a positive integer. We want to color the sides of \( P \) in 3 colors such that each color is used at least once. Additionally, from any point external to \( P \), at most 2 different colors on \( P \) can be seen (ignoring the vertices of \( P \), which are considered colorless). Find the largest positive integer \( n \) for which such a coloring is possible.

<think>
Okay, let's tackle this problem. It's about coloring the sides of a regular (2n+1)-gon with three colors, each color used at least once. The key condition is that from any external point, you can see at most two different colors. Hmm, interesting.

First, I need to visualize a regular polygon with an odd number of sides. Since it's (2n+1)-gon, the number of sides is odd, so like 3,5,7 etc. The coloring has to be such that each color is used at least once. The main constraint is that from any point outside the polygon, looking at it, you can't see more than two colors. 

Wait, so when you're looking from an external point, you see some sides of the polygon, right? Since it's a regular polygon, the sides are all the same length and angles. But how exactly would the visibility work here? Maybe the idea is that when you're outside the polygon, the sides that are visible from that point form a contiguous segment along the perimeter? Hmm, but actually, from a point outside, you can see two adjacent sides at each vertex, but maybe depending on where the point is, you might see multiple sides? Wait, maybe it's more straightforward.

Let me think. For a convex polygon, like a regular polygon, any external point can see the polygon as a star-shaped figure, but actually, it's a convex polygon, so from any external point, you can see exactly two sides? Wait, no. If you're far away, you might see more than two sides, but when you're close, you can only see two sides adjacent to each vertex. Hmm, maybe the problem is considering the sides that are visible from an external direction. Wait, perhaps the sides that are 'visible' from an external point are those that lie in the direction where you can see them without being blocked by the polygon itself.

Wait, perhaps the key idea is that from any direction (i.e., any point on the line at infinity), the polygon subtends an angle, and along that angle, the sides that are on the edge of the polygon from that direction must have at most two colors. Alternatively, maybe it's simpler to think that the polygon is in the plane, and an external point can be anywhere outside. The problem says "from any point external to P", so every possible external point must satisfy that the sides visible from that point don't have more than two colors. 

Hmm, so if you are standing outside the polygon and looking at it, the sides you can see form an arc of the polygon's perimeter, and the colors of those sides must not include more than two colors. Since the polygon is regular, maybe the coloring has to be such that any consecutive arc of the polygon's perimeter (as seen from some direction) can't have three different colors. Wait, but how is the visibility determined?

Alternatively, perhaps the condition is that when looking from any external point, the sides that are on the "front" of the polygon (i.e., the sides that are not hidden from view) can only have at most two different colors. Since the polygon is convex, from any external point, you can see at most a contiguous set of edges? Let me recall that in a convex polygon, the visibility from an external point is a single interval on the polygon's perimeter. So, each external point sees a contiguous arc of the polygon's sides. Therefore, the condition would be that every possible contiguous arc of sides must not contain all three colors. Wait, but that seems too strict, because if the arc is the entire polygon, which is a circle? Wait no, a polygon isn't a circle. The entire polygon is not an arc. Wait, actually, the polygon is a closed shape, so from some external points, maybe you can see almost the entire polygon except a small part?

Hmm, maybe I need to think differently. Let me look up "visibility of polygons from external points". But since I can't do that, I have to proceed logically.

Alternatively, maybe the problem is referring to that for any external direction, the sides that are visible along that direction must have at most two colors. If you consider the polygon as a silhouette, then looking at it from different angles, the sides that are on the "edge" of the silhouette must have at most two colors. So perhaps for each edge of the polygon, the two adjacent edges can't both have a third color? Hmm, not sure.

Alternatively, maybe the requirement is that if you take any side of the polygon, and look at it from the outside, the two adjacent sides to that side must not form a trio of colors. Wait, the problem says "from any point external to P, at most 2 different colors on P can be seen (ignoring the vertices of P, which are considered colorless)". So maybe when looking from a point outside, the sides that are visible from that point must form a set that contains at most two colors. Wait, but then the condition would be stronger: that any contiguous arc of edges must have at most two colors, but that might not be exactly what the problem states. The problem says "from any point external to P, at most 2 different colors on P can be seen". Since the polygon is convex, the set of edges visible from an external point is exactly a contiguous arc of edges, depending on where the point is located. So, the problem requires that for any contiguous arc of edges, the colors present in that arc must not include all three colors. Wait, but that would require that in any arc of edges, there are at most two colors. But the problem says "each color is used at least once". So we need a 3-coloring where every arc of edges (no matter how long or where it is) can only contain two colors, but all three colors are present somewhere.

Just in case, let us remind you that a clique in a non-directed graph is a subset of the vertices of a graph, such that any two vertices of this subset are connected by an edge. In particular, an empty set of vertexes and a set consisting of a single vertex, are cliques.

Let's define a divisibility graph for a set of positive integers A = {a1, a2, ..., an} as follows. The vertices of the given graph are numbers from set A, and two numbers ai and aj (i ≠ j) are connected by an edge if and only if either ai is divisible by aj, or aj is divisible by ai.

You are given a set of non-negative integers A. Determine the size of a maximum clique in a divisibility graph for set A.
Input

The first line contains integer n (1 ≤ n ≤ 106), that sets the size of set A.

The second line contains n distinct positive integers a1, a2, ..., an (1 ≤ ai ≤ 106) — elements of subset A. The numbers in the line follow in the ascending order.

Output

Print a single number — the maximum size of a clique in a divisibility graph for set A.

Examples

Input

8
3 4 6 8 10 18 21 24


Output

3

Note

In the first sample test a clique of size 3 is, for example, a subset of vertexes {3, 6, 18}. A clique of a larger size doesn't exist in this graph.

   338	    def select_file(self, path: Union[str, Path]) -> Self:
   339	        """
   340	        Manually select a file from it's path. No verification on the extension is performed.
   341	
   342	        Args:
   343	            path: the path to the file
   344	        """
   345	        # cast to Path
   346	        path = Path(path)
   347	
   348	        # test file existence
   349	        if not path.is_file():
   350	            raise Exception(f"{path} is not a file")
   351	
   352	        # set the menu to the folder of the file
   353	        self._on_file_select({"new": path.parent})
   354

   356	        self._on_file_select({"new": path})
   357

   359	
   360	    def _on_file_select(self, change: dict) -> Self:
   361	        """
   362	        Dispatch the behavior between file selection and folder change.
   363	        """
   364	        if not change["new"]:
   365	            return self
   366	
   367	        new_value = Path(change["new"])
   368	
   369	        if new_value.is_dir():
   370	            self.folder = new_value
   371	            self._change_folder()
   372	
   373	        elif new_value.is_file():
   374	            self.file = str(new_value)
   375	
   376	        return self
   377	
   378	    @sd.switch("indeterminate", on_widgets=["loading"])
   379	    def _change_folder(self) -> None:
   380	        """
   381	        Change the target folder.
   382	        """
   383	        # get the items
   384	        items = self._get_items()
   385	
   386	        # reset files
   387	        # this is reseting the scroll to top without using js scripts
   388	        self.file_list.children[0].children = []
   389	
   390	        # set the new files
   391	        self.file_list.children[0].children = items
   392	
   393	        return
   394	
   395	    def _get_items(self) -> List[v.ListItem]:
   396	        """
   397	        Create the list of items inside the folder.
   398	
   399	        Returns:
   400	            list of items inside the selected folder
   401	        """
   402	        folder = self.folder
   403	
   404	        list_dir = [el for el in folder.glob("*/") if not el.name.startswith(".")]
   405	
   406	        if self.extentions:
   407	            list_dir = [
   408	                el for el in list_dir if el.is_dir() or el.suffix in self.extentions
   409	            ]
   410	
   411	        folder_list = []
   412	        file_list = []
   413	
   414	        for el in list_dir:
   415	
   416	            if el.is_dir():
   417	                icon = self.ICON_STYLE[""]["icon"]
   418	                color = self.ICON_STYLE[""]["color"][v.theme.dark]
   419	            elif el.suffix in self.ICON_STYLE.keys():
   420	                icon = self.ICON_STYLE[el.suffix]["icon"]
   421	                color = self.ICON_STYLE[el.suffix]["color"][v.theme.dark]
   422	            else:
   423	                icon = self.ICON_STYLE["DEFAULT"]["icon"]
   424	                color = self.ICON_STYLE["DEFAULT"]["color"][v.theme.dark]
   425	
   426	            children = [
   427	                v.ListItemAction(children=[v.Icon(color=color, children=[icon])]),
   428	                v.ListItemContent(
   429	                    children=[v.ListItemTitle(children=[el.stem + el.suffix])]
   430	                ),
   431	            ]
   432	
   433	            if el.is_dir():
   434	                folder_list.append(v.ListItem(value=str(el), children=children))
   435	            else:
   436	                file_size = su.get_file_size(el)
   437	                children.append(
   438	                    v.ListItemActionText(class_="ml-1", children=[file_size])
   439	                )
   440	                file_list.append(v.ListItem(value=str(el), children=children))
   441	
   442	        folder_list = humansorted(folder_list, key=lambda x: x.value)
   443	        file_list = humansorted(file_list, key=lambda x: x.value)
   444	
   445	        parent_item = v.ListItem(
   446	            value=str(folder.parent),
   447	            children=[
   448	                v.ListItemAction(
   449	                    children=[
   450	                        v.Icon(
   451	                            color=self.ICON_STYLE["PARENT"]["color"][v.theme.dark],
   452	                            children=[self.ICON_STYLE["PARENT"]["icon"]],
   453	                        )
   454	                    ]
   455	                ),
   456	                v.ListItemContent(
   457	                    children=[v.ListItemTitle(children=[f".. /{folder.parent.stem}"])]
   458	                ),
   459	            ],
   460	        )
   461	
   462	        folder_list.extend(file_list)
   463	        folder_list.insert(0, parent_item)
   464	
   465	        return folder_list
   466	
   467	    def _on_reload(self, *args) -> None:
   468	
   469	        # force the update of the current folder
   470	        self._change_folder()
   471	
   472	        return

Wait, exactly! So here's the idea. 

Initialize an array 'dp' where dp[x] (for x in the array) will store the length of the maximum chain ending at x. Also, since the elements are unique, for x not in the array, dp[x] is 0 (or undefined).

But since the elements can be up to 1e6, an array of size 1e6+1 could be manageable. 

Let's formalize it:

Initialize dp[x] = 1 for all x in A (since each number forms a chain of length 1 by itself).

Then for each number x in A sorted order (so processed in increasing order):

For each divisor d of x (excluding x itself):

   if d is present in A, then dp[x] = max(dp[x], dp[d] + 1).

This kind of coloring is possible only if the entire edge sequence can be partitioned into two colors except for one edge of the third color inserted in such a way that no interval includes that edge and edges of both other colors.

Then, for each x in A (in any order, since when processing x's multiples, the current dp[x] is already set):

Wait the processing order is vital. Because we must process x in order of increasing. Since the input array is already sorted, processing them in the given order ensures that. 

Because when handling the multiples of a number 'x', all multiples that are larger (processed later) have their DP's updated by x's current DP (which is correct). 

Thus, the process must iterate over x in increasing order, so that when we process x's multiples which are larger than x and may have not been processed yet.

Thus, we process all elements in increasing order (so the input order).

Thus, the steps in code would be:

Read n.

Read array a, which is in sorted order.

max_A = a[-1]

initialize present array (size max_A+1) to false.

initialize dp array (size max_A+1) to zero.

for each x in a:

   present[x] = True

   dp[x] =1 

then for each x in a:

   current dp for x is already set, so for all multiples of x, we check if the multiple m is present:

   for m in 2*x, 3*x, ... up to max_A:

      if present[m]:

         if dp[m] < dp[x]+1:

             dp[m] = dp[x]+1

Now the key thing is to loop over the multiples.

The problem is the loop for m. How to generate all multiples of x starting from 2*x up to max_A.

This can be implemented as:

for multiple in range(2, max_A //x +1):

   m = multiple *x

   if m >max_A:

       break.

   if present[m]:

       if dp[m] < dp[x]+1:

           dp[m] = dp[x]+1

Thus, for example, for x=3:

the outer loop is for multiple in 2,3, 4,... up to floor( (1e6)/3 )

then m=3*2=6, 3*3=9, etc. 

Thus, for each x, the loop runs up to M/x iterations. 

This is O(M log M).

In terms of coding in C++, here are steps:

Read n.

Read the array, sort it (though the input says 'ascending' so perhaps no need, but need to confirm the problem statement).

Problem says the elements are given in "ascending order". So the input is already sorted, so we don't need to sort again, just read the array.

First line: n.

Second line: a list of sorted numbers.

We can read them as a vector (in C++:

vector<int> a; 

then read them.

Then compute max_A as a.back().

Initialize present array (array of booleans with max_A+1 elements).

Initialize dp array as an array of integers with max_A+1 elements.

Then, loop over a's elements, set their present to true and dp to 1.

Then loop for each element x in a:

   For each multiple from 2* x, 3*x, up to max_A:

      m = multiple_of_x.

      if present[m]:

         dp[m] = max(dp[m], dp[x]+1) → but in the algorithm we suggested previously, it's a simple assignment because the first increment.

Wait perhaps the previous idea is better: 

for a given m, it can have multiple divisors in the array. So, whenever any of the divisors x are processed (but in order of x increasing), we update dp[m].

The DP of m is equal to the maximum of all dp[x] +1 where x divides m (and present). So, this way, when we process x, m will get the max between its previous and dp[x]+1.

Thus, in code, it is better to do:

if (dp[m] < dp[x]+1):

     dp[m]=dp[x]+1;

That way, it only improves m's DP if the current x gives a better option.

Thus:

for (int x : a){

    for(int multiple= 2; multiple*x <= max_A; multiple++ ){

        int m = multiple *x;

        if (present[m]){

            if( dp[m] < dp[x]+1){

                dp[m] = dp[x]+1;

            }

        }

    }

}

Wait no, actually, the outer loop is over x in the array.

Yes.

Now the code's steps are correct.

Wait, but for the code:

Wait in code:

for (each x in a) :

    for multiple starting at 2 to m_max.

    where m = x * multiple.

Thus, the code for the multiples is as follows.

Because, for multiplier in 2, 3, …, floor(max_A/x). 

But in code:

start from 2, and while x * multiplier <= max_A.

So:

for (auto x : a) {

    for( int m = 2 * x; m <= max_A; m +=x ){

        if (present[m]) {

            if(dp[m] < dp[x] +1) {

               dp[m] = dp[x] +1;

            }

       }

   }

}

This is more efficient. Because adding x each time allows to step over all m's which are multiples of x from 2x step by x until reach max_A. 

This way the inner loop is m steps each equal to x.

For example, for x=3:

start with m=6 → yes, then 9 (if within), 12, etc. 

The code loops through all multiples of x starting from 2x in increments of x. 

That's the most efficient way to iterate through all multiples of x beyond x itself.

Ah, that's better than the loops I thought about earlier. 

Yes, this loop structure:

for (x in a) {

   current =2*x;

   step x each time until current <= max_A.

}

That reduces exactly all multiples starting from 2x in steps of x.

Perfect. Thus, the code is now feasible. 

Thus, this code is O(N * (average number of multiples beyond x that exist)) but more accurately O( sum_{x in A} ( (max_A/x) )

Which as discussed earlier is acceptable.

Another approach: If I consider the coordinates. Let me place the trapezoid on a coordinate system. Let's say the longer base of 50 cm is on the x-axis from (0,0) to (50,0). The other base (shorter one) is parallel, so it will be somewhere above, say from (a,h) to (50 - a, h), where 'h' is the height and 'a' is the horizontal distance from the left end of the longer base to the left end of the shorter base. Since the legs are 30 cm each, the legs connect (0,0) to (a,h) and (50,0) to (50 - a, h). The length of the leg is 30 cm, so using the distance formula:

(50 - a)^2 + h^2 = 1600.

1) a² + h² = 900

2) (50 - a)^2 + h² = 1600

If I subtract equation 1 from equation 2, I can eliminate h²:

(50 - a)^2 - a² = 1600 - 900

Expanding (50 - a)^2: 2500 - 100a + a² - a² = 2500 -100a = 700

</think>To find the area of the isosceles trapezoid, we will use the formula for the area of a trapezoid: 
\[ \text{Area} = \frac{1}{2} \times (a + b) \times h, \]
where \(a\) and \(b\) are the lengths of the two bases, and \(h\) is the height. Given:
- The longer base \(a = 50\) cm,
- The legs are 30 cm each,
- The diagonals are 40 cm each.

First, we need to determine the height \(h\) and the length of the shorter base \(b\).

### Step 1: Determine the height \(h\)

Using the Pythagorean theorem for the leg from \((0, 0)\) to \((a, h)\):
\[ a^2 + h^2 = 30^2 \]
\[ a^2 + h^2 = 900 \quad \text{(Equation 1)} \]

Using the Pythagorean theorem for the diagonal from \((0, 0)\) to \((50 - a, h)\):
\[ (50 - a)^2 + h^2 = 40^2 \]
\[ (50 - a)^2 + h^2 = 1600 \quad \text{(Equation 2)} \]

### Step 2: Solve for \(a\)

\[ (50 - a)^2 + h^2 - (a^2 + h^2) = 1600 - 900 \]
\[ (50 - a)^2 - a^2 = 700 \]
\[ 2500 - 100a + a^2 - a^2 = 700 \]
\[ 2500 - 100a = 700 \]
\[ 100a = 1800 \]
\[ a = 18 \]

To solve this problem, we need to determine the maximum size of a clique in a divisibility graph for a given set of integers. A clique in this context is a subset of numbers where each pair of numbers either divides or is divisible by the other. The optimal approach leverages dynamic programming and the properties of divisibility to efficiently compute the result.

### Approach
1. **Problem Analysis**: 
   - A clique in the divisibility graph is a subset of numbers where each number divides another number in the subset or is divided by another number in the subset.
   - The problem can be reduced to finding the longest chain of numbers where each number divides the next one. This is analogous to finding the longest chain in a partially ordered set (poset) based on divisibility.

2. **Dynamic Programming Insight**:
   - For each number `x` in the set, the length of the longest chain ending at `x` (denoted as `dp[x]`) can be determined by considering all divisors of `x` that are also present in the set.
   - To avoid explicitly checking all divisors for each number, we use an efficient approach by iterating through multiples of each number. This allows us to update the longest chain for all multiples as we process each number in increasing order.

3. **Algorithm**:
   - **Initialization**: 
     - Use an array `present` to track which numbers are in the input set.
     - Use an array `dp` where `dp[x]` will store the length of the longest chain ending at `x`.
   - **Processing**:
     - For each number `x` in the input set (sorted in ascending order), iterate through all multiples of `x` (starting from `2*x` to the maximum value in the set). For each multiple `m` present in the set, update `dp[m]` to be the maximum of its current value and `dp[x] + 1`.

### Solution Code
```cpp
#include <bits/stdc++.h>
using namespace std;

int main() {
    ios_base::sync_with_stdio(0);
    cin.tie(0);
    cout.tie(0);

    int n;
    cin >> n;
    vector<int> a(n);
    for (int i = 0; i < n; ++i) {
        cin >> a[i];
    }

    if (n == 0) {
        cout << 0 << endl;
        return 0;
    }

    int max_A = a.back();
    vector<bool> present(max_A + 1, false);
    vector<int> dp(max_A + 1, 0);

    for (int x : a) {
        present[x] = true;
        dp[x] = 1;
    }

    for (int x : a) {
        for (int m = 2 * x; m <= max_A; m += x) {
            if (present[m]) {
                if (dp[m] < dp[x] + 1) {
                    dp[m] = dp[x] + 1;
                }
            }
        }
    }

    int ans = 0;
    for (int x : a) {
        ans = max(ans, dp[x]);
    }

    cout << ans << endl;

    return 0;
}
```

### Explanation
1. **Reading Input**: The input is read and stored in an array `a`, which is already guaranteed to be in ascending order.
2. **Initialization**:
   - `present` array marks the presence of numbers in the input set.
   - `dp` array initializes each number's chain length to 1 (since each number forms a chain of length 1 by itself).
3. **Processing Each Number**:
   - For each number `x`, iterate over all multiples starting from `2*x` up to the maximum value in the set. For each multiple `m` that is present in the input set, update the chain length at `m` using the chain length from `x`.
4. **Result Calculation**: The maximum value in the `dp` array for numbers present in the input set is the size of the largest clique.

This approach efficiently computes the result by leveraging the properties of divisibility and using dynamic programming with an optimized loop structure, ensuring that each number is processed in linear time relative to its multiples. The complexity is managed to be approximately O(N log N) by iterating over multiples of each number, making it feasible for large input sizes.<#sep#>pal_ui.bin.sepal_ipyvuetify:main"

\[ 18^2 + h^2 = 900 \]
\[ 324 + h^2 = 900 \]
\[ h^2 = 576 \]
\[ h = 24 \]

### Step 4: Determine the shorter base \(b\)

\[ b = (50 - a) - a = 50 - 2a \]
\[ b = 50 - 2 \times 18 = 50 - 36 = 14 \]

\[ \text{Area} = \frac{1}{2} \times (a + b) \times h \]
\[ \text{Area} = \frac{1}{2} \times (50 + 14) \times 24 \]
\[ \text{Area} = \frac{1}{2} \times 64 \times 24 \]
\[ \text{Area} = 32 \times 24 \]
\[ \text{Area} = 768 \]

One can see that with fixed sequences x_i, y_i, a_i the tuples f^{(k)} starting from some index will repeat tuples with smaller indices. Input

The first line contains one integer n (1 ≤ n ≤ 2 ⋅ 10^5) — the number of elements in the tuple.

The second line contains n space separated prime numbers — the modules p_1, p_2, …, p_n (2 ≤ p_i ≤ 2 ⋅ 10^6).

Output

Print one integer — the maximum number of different tuples modulo 10^9 + 7.

Examples

Input

Output

In the second example we can choose next parameters: a = [1, 1, 2], b = [1, 1, 0], x = [0, 0, 1].

   321	        Clear the File selection and move to the root folder.

   337	
   338	    def select_file(self, path: Union[str, Path]) -> Self:
   339	        """
   340	        Manually select a file from it's path. No verification on the extension is performed.
   341	
   342	        Args:
   343	            path: the path to the file
   344	        """
   345	        # cast to Path
   346	        path = Path(path)
   347	
   348	        # test file existence
   349	        if not path.is_file():
   350	            raise Exception(f"{path} is not a file")
   351	
   352	        # set the menu to the folder of the file
   353	        self._on_file_select({"new": path.parent})
   354	
   355	        # select the appropriate file
   356	        self._on_file_select({"new": path})
   357	
   358	        return self
   359	
   360	    def _on_file_select(self, change: dict) -> Self:
   361	        """
   362	        Dispatch the behavior between file selection and folder change.
   363	        """
   364	        if not change["new"]:
   365	            return self
   366	
   367	        new_value = Path(change["new"])
   368	
   369	        if new_value.is_dir():
   370	            self.folder = new_value
   371	            self._change_folder()
   372	
   373	        elif new_value.is_file():
   374	            self.file = str(new_value)
   375	
   376	        return self
   377	
   378	    @sd.switch("indeterminate", on_widgets=["loading"])
   379	    def _change_folder(self) -> None:
   380	        """
   381	        Change the target folder.
   382	        """
   383	        # get the items
   384	        items = self._get_items()
   385	
   386	        # reset files
   387	        # this is reseting the scroll to top without using js scripts
   388	        self.file_list.children[0].children = []
   389	
   390	        # set the new files
   391	        self.file_list.children[0].children = items
   392	
   393	        return
   394	
   395	    def _get_items(self) -> List[v.ListItem]:
   396	        """
   397	        Create the list of items inside the folder.
   398	
   399	        Returns:
   400	            list of items inside the selected folder
   401	        """
   402	        folder = self.folder
   403	
   404	        list_dir = [el for el in folder.glob("*/") if not el.name.startswith(".")]
   405	
   406	        if self.extentions:
   407	            list_dir = [
   408	                el for el in list_dir if el.is_dir() or el.suffix in self.extentions
   409	            ]
   410	
   411	        folder_list = []
   412	        file_list = []
   413	
   414	        for el in list_dir:
   415	
   416	            if el.is_dir():
   417	                icon = self.ICON_STYLE[""]["icon"]
   418	                color = self.ICON_STYLE[""]["color"][v.theme.dark]
   419	            elif el.suffix in self.ICON_STYLE.keys():
   420	                icon = self.ICON_STYLE[el.suffix]["icon"]
   421	                color = self.ICON_STYLE[el.suffix]["color"][v.theme.dark]
   422	            else:
   423	                icon = self.ICON_STYLE["DEFAULT"]["icon"]
   424	                color = self.ICON_STYLE["DEFAULT"]["color"][v.theme.dark]
   425	
   426	            children = [
   427	                v.ListItemAction(children=[v.Icon(color=color, children=[icon])]),
   428	                v.ListItemContent(
   429	                    children=[v.ListItemTitle(children=[el.stem + el.suffix])]
   430	                ),
   431	            ]
   432	
   433	            if el.is_dir():
   434	                folder_list.append(v.ListItem(value=str(el), children=children))
   435	            else:
   436	                file_size = su.get_file_size(el)
   437	                children.append(
   438	                    v.ListItemActionText(class_="ml-1", children=[file_size])
   439	                )
   440	                file_list.append(v.ListItem(value=str(el), children=children))
   441	
   442	        folder_list = humansorted(folder_list, key=lambda x: x.value)
   443	        file_list = humansorted(file_list, key=lambda x: x.value)
   444	
   445	        parent_item = v.ListItem(
   446	            value=str(folder.parent),
   447	            children=[
   448	                v.ListItemAction(
   449	                    children=[
   450	                        v.Icon(
   451	                            color=self.ICON_STYLE["PARENT"]["color"][v.theme.dark],
   452	                            children=[self.ICON_STYLE["PARENT"]["icon"]],
   453	                        )
   454	                    ]
   455	                ),
   456	                v.ListItemContent(
   457	                    children=[v.ListItemTitle(children=[f".. /{folder.parent.stem}"])]
   458	                ),
   459	            ],
   460	        )
   461	
   462	        folder_list.extend(file_list)
   463	        folder_list.insert(0, parent_item)
   464	
   465	        return folder_list
   466	
   467	    def _on_reload(self, *args) -> None:
   468	
   469	        # force the update of the current folder
   470	        self._change_folder()
   471	
   472	        return

So in essence, the algorithm for each prime p (with multiplicity C):

The best is to:

- Use one occurrence of p to choose period p: contributing p to the LCM.

- Use the remaining occurrence(s) of p to choose periods from divisors of (p-1), which contain factors not present in p (like 2 in case of p=3).

Thus, the approach is for each prime p:

- The first use (one of its instances) gives us p in LCM.

- The remaining instances of p can choose to contribute the multiplicative group's divisors in any way that maximizes the total LCM.

Hence, the key is to maximize the LCM by

- One of them is taken to contribute a factor of p.

- The remaining duplicates can contribute factors using the divisors of p-1, but ideally large numbers.

Hence, the problem reduces to, for each p:

- Use one of its occurrences to have period p, providing p to LCM.

- Assign the other (C-1) occurrences of p to periods of divisors of (p-1).

Second term: (0.0001 +0.0000001)/(0.0001 +0.0000002)= ~0.00010001/0.00010002≈0.9999.

Third term: (0.0000001 +1)/(0.0000001 +2)≈1.0000001/2.0000001≈0.5.

Sum≈0.9999 +0.9999 +0.5≈2.4998, which is very close to 2.5. So it seems that when two variables are taken to approach 0 and one is fixed (or taken to infinity, but in such a way that two variables go to 0), the sum approaches 5/2. So this seems like the supremum.

Therefore, the minimal value of D is 5/2, as the sum can approach 2.5 but never exceed it. Hence, D=5/2.
</think>To find the minimal value of \( D \) such that the inequality
\[
\frac{a+b}{a+2b} + \frac{b+c}{b+2c} + \frac{c+a}{c+2a} < D
\]
holds for all \( a, b, c \in \mathbb{R^+} \), we need to determine the supremum of the expression.

### Step 1: Analyze the individual terms
Each term in the expression \(\frac{x+y}{x+2y}\) is bounded between \(\frac{1}{2}\) and \(1\). Specifically:
- When \( x \gg y \), \(\frac{x+y}{x+2y} \approx 1\).
- When \( y \gg x \), \(\frac{x+y}{x+2y} \approx \frac{1}{2}\).

### Step 2: Evaluate the expression for specific values
Consider the case where \( a = b = c \):
\[
\frac{a+b}{a+2b} + \frac{b+c}{b+2c} + \frac{c+a}{c+2a} = \frac{2a}{3a} + \frac{2a}{3a} + \frac{2a}{3a} = 3 \cdot \frac{2}{3} = 2.
\]
Thus, the sum is 2 in this case.

### Step 3: Investigate the behavior as variables approach limits
Consider the scenario where \( a \) is fixed and \( b, c \) approach 0:
\[
\frac{a+b}{a+2b} \approx 1, \quad \frac{b+c}{b+2c} \approx \frac{b}{2b} = \frac{1}{2}, \quad \frac{c+a}{c+2a} \approx \frac{a}{2a} = \frac{1}{2}.
\]
So the sum is:
\[
1 + \frac{1}{2} + \frac{1}{2} = 2.
\]

### Step 4: Investigate the behavior when one variable is large
Consider the scenario where \( a \to \infty \) and \( b, c \) are fixed:
\[
\frac{a+b}{a+2b} \approx 1, \quad \frac{b+c}{b+2c} \text{ is a constant}, \quad \frac{c+a}{c+2a} \approx \frac{1}{2}.
\]
Thus, the sum is:
\[
1 + \text{constant} + \frac{1}{2}.
\]
The constant \(\frac{b+c}{b+2c}\) can be maximized to 1 when \( c \to 0 \):
\[
1 + 1 + \frac{1}{2} = \frac{5}{2}.
\]

### Step 5: Confirm the supremum
We need to confirm that the expression can approach \(\frac{5}{2}\) but never exceed it. Consider the case where \( a = 1 \), \( b \to 0 \), and \( c = k b \) with \( k \to 0 \):
\[
\frac{1+b}{1+2b} \approx 1, \quad \frac{b+kb}{b+2kb} = \frac{1+k}{1+2k} \approx 1, \quad \frac{kb+1}{kb+2} \approx \frac{1}{2}.
\]
Thus, the sum is:
\[
1 + 1 + \frac{1}{2} = \frac{5}{2}.
\]

### Conclusion
The minimal value of \( D \) such that the inequality holds for all \( a, b, c \in \mathbb{R^+} \) is:
\[
\boxed{\frac{5}{2}}.
